# Provenance of the handing-over-to-readers rules

One line per shipped rule of `references/rules.md`, keyed `R-n` by the operator's number, one per bullet of its "What the research adds", and one per clause of `references/explaining.md`, `references/explainer.md`, `references/media.md` and `references/page.md`, keyed `Explaining:`, `Explainer:`, `Media:` and `Page:`; the lines keyed `Intake:`, `Gate:` and `Feedback:` cover `references/intake.md`, the ledger with its gate and the usage journal, and the line keyed `Evidence:` a clause of `references/evidence.md`. The lines on the explanation page cite the research note `research/2026-11-14_explanation-page-evidence.md` by section, and the lines that entered with the review of that page by four of its readers cite `research/2026-11-22_explainer-review-evidence.md`. The clause lines cite the research note `research/2026-11-07_report-forms-evidence.md` by section: § The readings holds what the operator said at each of his seven readings of report forms (Reading 1 … Reading 7), § Measurements the counts taken on the texts, § What the literature adds the opened sources. Each line holds the sources the rule or clause was drawn from with their locators, its formulation and its caveat, then the tests and documents found for it, then its record in the format of `../../scientific-method/references/record-format.md`. A line whose claim waits for its test is its owner's decision, and its record names the claim as `H-<id of the line>` in `pending-evidence.md`. `citations.json` holds the words and figures the lines quote from their sources, one entry per quote. A source is `first-hand` when its text (abstract, HTML or full text, as the pointer says) was opened; a figure seen only in a search summary is `relay` and carries no rule. `README n` is entry n of the repository `README.md` §References, verified first-hand by the output-style programme. The formulation of every rule is the operator's list of 27 rules; a second formulation is named where one exists.

- **R-1** · Rao & Daumé III 2018, *Learning to Ask Good Questions*, arXiv:1805.04655 (PDF: "a good question is one whose expected answer will be useful") · the operator's list · eval client-question-list E3–E7 · the paper values a question by the use of its answer; it does not test the definition of a candidate · Rao & Daumé III Learning to Ask Good Questions arXiv:1805.04655 §5.2, Table 2 — a model that ranks the ten clarification questions retrieved for a StackExchange post by the expected utility of their answers picked the question experts marked best more often than neural baselines (p@1 27.7 against 25.2 for the strongest), a test of ranking candidates already retrieved, not of where candidates come from; Vijayvargiya, Viswanathan & Neubig Asking What Matters: Reward-Driven Clarification arXiv:2604.14624 §6.4, Table 3 — on 500 underspecified SWE-bench Verified issues with a simulated user, an 8B question module trained to ask for information the user can supply and that bears on success resolved 36.8% with 3.0 questions against 35.6% with 5.1 for GPT-5, the answerability of the two sets alike (.373 against .369) and the source naming no test; Jiang, Pahwa, Zhang, Zhang, Xia & Soatto Learning When Human Decisions Matter arXiv:2611.05814 §1 — holds that humans supply private context, authority or judgments unavailable to the agent, and takes the candidate consultation points as already identified, its tests comparing ways of spending a budget of questions among them; Grunde-McLaughlin et al. Agentic Path Maps arXiv:2611.04183 §6.1, Table 2 — 48 novice analysts reviewing a data-analysis agent's result put less weight on an extreme output when shown three or nine analyses of the question than when shown one analysis, picked for its minimum or maximum output to isolate anchoring (effect -0.63 [-0.99, -0.24], posterior probability 0.9992 in a Bayesian linear mixed model), at a higher intrinsic cognitive load (0.40 [0.10, 0.70]) and a higher extraneous cognitive load (0.31 [-0.06, 0.67]), under other conditions: novice analysts estimating a relationship from the analyses shown to them, not an addressee answering an agent's questions or weighing the decisions it took itself · kind: decision · owner: the repository owner · goal: the question list holds only the points where the work needs the addressee's decision or data he alone holds, and the agent's own choices are taken and recorded rather than asked · hypothesis: H-R-1

Mayer, Heiser & Lonn had 78 college students watch a narrated animation of how lightning forms with or without six additional sentences of entertaining but conceptually irrelevant information, and with them retention was 3.76 against 5.07 idea units (F(1,74) = 14.39, p < .001) and transfer 3.05 against 3.95 solutions (F(1, 74) = 5.67, p < .05), effect sizes .56 and .55, in the claim's direction under other conditions: a system-paced lesson, and added sentences chosen to interest the learner rather than surplus of the matter; Mayer, Bove, Bryman, Mars & Tapangco report from three experiments with college students that a summary of short captions with simple illustrations was recalled and solved transfer problems from as well as or better than the full text with or without the summary, and that adding text to the summary reduced its effectiveness, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction under other conditions: a science lesson, and captions with illustrations in place of prose; Rey meta-analysed 39 experimental effects and reports a significant seductive detail effect, small to medium on retention and medium on transfer, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction under other conditions: interesting but irrelevant details in instructional messages; Sundararajan & Adesope report that including seductive details in learning material can hinder learning, with findings mixed on the degree and the effect moderated by design and method, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction under the same other conditions; Charney, Reder & Wells gave 40 inexperienced computer users an elaborated manual of 11,000 words or an unelaborated one of 3,500 words to read for forty-five minutes before file tasks done without it, 20 of them told the tasks in advance and 20 not, half of each group with each manual, and both manuals gave about the same share of tasks completed, .80 against .80 among those told and .85 against .76 among those not told; those not told took 29.4 against 40.2 minutes and 76.8 against 101.8 commands with the elaborated manual, and those told took 33.5 against 36.1 minutes and 95.8 against 94.2 commands, which the authors read as much more efficient with the unelaborated manual, the chapter naming no test and referring to Reder, Charney & Morgan 1986 for the study; in Study 2, 40 novice and 40 experienced users, none told the tasks, read one of four versions crossing rich or sparse procedural with rich or sparse conceptual elaborations, and with rich procedural elaborations, examples of correct commands and details of notational conventions, they took 37.4 and 37.7 minutes and 71.7 and 73.7 commands, with rich and sparse conceptual elaborations, against 43.5 and 45.9 minutes and 88.7 and 92.4 commands with sparse procedural ones, called significantly worse with no test named, while conceptual elaborations had no effect, so that cutting the conceptual elaborations cost nothing and cutting the procedural ones cost time and commands, under other conditions: time and commands for a skill applied from a manual taken away before the tasks in place of the points of a report recalled, elaborations the reader used to apply the commands in place of sentences that answer none of his questions, and the effect of length bounded by whether the reader knows his task in advance; Steyvers et al. had participants judge the probability that an LLM's answer to a multiple-choice or short-answer question was correct, and in Experiments 2a to 2c, of 60, 60 and 59 participants, long explanations of median 115, 64 and 95 words raised their confidence above short explanations of median 34, 24 and 24 words (BF = 25, the three experiments combined), while the mean participant AUC was 0.54 with the long explanations and 0.57 with uncertainty-only responses of median 9 words, which kept the answer and its expression of uncertainty and dropped the rationale (BF = .23, moderate evidence for no difference on the source's scale), the longer text giving no better discrimination than the answer with its uncertainty alone, and §3.1 giving no AUC for the short explanations, under other conditions: quiz answers judged for correctness in place of a report, confidence and telling right answers from wrong in place of retelling, long explanations compared with responses stripped of their rationale and not with a text cut to the sentences the reader needs, and a Bayes factor from Bayesian t-tests in place of a test against chance; doi:10.1016/j.edurev.2012.05.003 and doi:10.1007/s10648-020-09522-4: experiments on seductive details in instructional messages, meta-analysed in 2012 and 2020; Charney, Reder & Wells: in Study 1, 40 inexperienced computer users, 20 told the file tasks in advance and 20 not, 10 per manual in each group, with PC-DOS manuals of 11,000 and 3,500 words read for forty-five minutes and taken away before the tasks, and in Study 2, 40 novice and 40 experienced computer users who had never used an IBM-PC, none told the tasks, with four versions of that manual, in a chapter of 1988 reporting studies of Reder, Charney & Morgan 1986; the two tests that told their effect from chance fixed the study time and measured recall for a memory test, so whether the reader reads a short report to its end or sooner is untested; the summaries ran to 800 and 1,000 words and the cut lesson was a narration, so the size of twenty to thirty sentences and about two hundred words is untested and rests on the operator's readings; Charney, Reder & Wells found the unelaborated manual worse for readers who did not know their tasks in advance and, by the authors' reading, more efficient for those who did, and the manuals cut of their examples of correct commands worse for readers not told the tasks, with no test named; Steyvers et al. compared long explanations with responses stripped of their rationale, not with a text cut to what the reader needs; and Friedman et al. found the shortest outputs worst at catching a wrong conclusion

- **Explaining: the cold read, what it shows and what it does not** · the measurement `evals/cold-read/`: four reports of `evals/runs/iteration-2` written with the skill, each with three planted omissions (the reader's decision, the cause of the problem, a sentence that introduces a thing), read blind by a fresh agent, one run a cell. `runs/iteration-3` (the shipped instructions, six questions): the decision marked in 4 of 4, the cause in 4 of 4, the introducing sentence in 0 of 4, nothing marked on the 4 intact reports. `runs/iteration-2` (the same questions, the instructions before the context audit's wording fixes): the same outcome in all sixteen cells. `runs/iteration-1` (the first prompt, five questions): 2 of 4 intact reports marked on a question, and 3 to 6 terms listed as unexplained on every intact report, which is why the terms change no exit code and the prompt names what is left out. `runs/iteration-4` and `runs/iteration-5` (the shipped instructions read by two other models): the decision and the cause in 4 of 4, the introducing sentence in 1 of 4, and one intact report marked on the question about what is unverified by each, which is why a mark is weighed against the text and not applied as a defect. `runs/iteration-6` (the instructions that add the retelling of a first part, the same six questions without a request to retell, read by Opus): the decision marked in 4 of 4, the cause in 4 of 4, the introducing sentence in 0 of 4, nothing marked on the 4 intact reports, the outcome of `runs/iteration-3` in all sixteen cells · a hands-on measurement on real reports (`research/2026-11-07_report-forms-evidence.md` § The cold read on real reports): 60 closing messages of one operator's sessions, 30 he asked back about and 30 he approved, one read each against the six default questions; - **Explaining: the terms of a compound condition are joined by written connectives** · the clause is in force as the decision of the repository's owner, and the sources below were found by a search of the literature; the definitions came from the same paper and not from code, the tooltip's own share of the bundle is not isolated, the glossary was prepended to the start of the paper and did not stand at its end, and the reading was of one PDF with a mouse; readers of an encyclopedia, a preview of an article's lead, and engagement counted with no measure of understanding; Ko, Myers, Coblenz & Aung *An Exploratory Study of How Developers Seek, Relate, and Collect Relevant Information during Software Maintenance Tasks* doi:10.1109/TSE.2006.116 abstract — developers given an unfamiliar program and two debugging and three enhancement tasks in 70 minutes in Eclipse spent on average 35 percent of their time on the mechanics of navigation within and between source files, the abstract giving no sample size; one IDE of 2006 and maintenance tasks, not the reading of an explanation; Bragdon, Zeleznik, Reiss, Karumuri, Cheung, Kaplan, Coleman, Adeputra & LaViola *Code Bubbles: a working set-based interface for code understanding and maintenance* doi:10.1145/1753326.1753706 abstract — code fragments shown side by side significantly improved code understanding time and cut navigation interactions against a widely used IDE in two controlled tasks, with no figures in the abstract; links from requirements and not from sentences, solutions sketched on paper, and the participants' experience not described in the abstract; *Providing Information About Implemented Algorithms Improves Program Comprehension: A Controlled Experiment* (EASE 2025) arXiv:2504.19225 abstract — 56 participants in two groups scored a median 6 points (about 23 %) higher on comprehension exercises with code labelled by the algorithm it implements (p = 0.040), at the same completion time (p = 0.991), the benefit largest at medium programming experience; a label that runs from code to the algorithm's name, the reverse of a line of pseudocode that names its method, in one study; Tan, Wagner & Treude *Detecting Outdated Code Element References in Software Repository Documentation* doi:10.1007/s10664-023-10397-6 (arXiv:2212.01479) § 1 Introduction and the results for the top1000 dataset — in the README and wiki pages of more than 3,000 GitHub projects, a reference counting as outdated when every instance of its code element was deleted (manual annotation, kappa 0.92), 28.9 % of the most popular projects (265 of 918), 19.2 % of their documents (1880 of 9784) and 3.9 % of their references (7910 of 201852) held one, 82.3 % of the projects had held one at some time, and such references typically stayed for years; - **Page: content shown on hover or focus opens on focus as on hover, stays under the pointer and closes on Escape** · in force as the decision of the repository's owner, which takes the criteria below as written; the sources were found by a search of the literature · W3C *Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG) 2.2*, W3C Recommendation, https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/ Success Criterion 1.4.13 Content on Hover or Focus (Level AA), the same criterion in WCAG 2.1 https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG21/ — content that pointer hover or keyboard focus brings up is dismissible, a mechanism closing it without moving hover or focus, hoverable, "the pointer can be moved over the additional content without the additional content disappearing", and persistent until the trigger is removed, the user dismisses it or its information ceases to be valid; the criterion names no key and no trigger, and the tooltip a browser draws for the title attribute lies outside it; W3C *Understanding SC 1.4.13: Content on Hover or Focus*, updated 12 July 2026, https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/content-on-hover-or-focus.html — the document that explains the criterion names both: "Content which can be triggered via pointer hover should also be able to be triggered by keyboard focus", and a way to dismiss the content without moving the mouse or the keyboard focus, "such as pressing the Esc key"; the opening on focus and the Escape key of the clause follow this document; W3C *WCAG 2.1* https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG21/ Success Criterion 2.1.1 Keyboard (Level A) — "All functionality of the content is operable through a keyboard interface without requiring specific timings for individual keystrokes", which a preview that opens on hover alone leaves unmet and one that opens on focus as well meets; WHATWG *HTML Living Standard* https://html.spec.whatwg.org/multipage/dom.html#the-title-attribute § 3.2.6.1 The title attribute — "Relying on the title attribute is currently discouraged as many user agents do not expose the attribute in an accessible manner as required by this specification", its example naming a tooltip that needs a mouse as one that excludes keyboard-only and touch-only users; W3C *Media Queries Level 4* https://www.w3.org/TR/mediaqueries-4/ § 7.2 Hover Capability — hover: none matches touchscreens and devices where hovering is inconvenient, and authors "should design layouts that do not depend on hovering to be fully usable"; § 7.3 All Available Interaction Capabilities — any-hover answers for the union of the capabilities of every pointing device, none only when no device can hover or there is none; the ordinary link that opens the panel on a screen whose pointer cannot hover follows these two sections · kind: norm · issuer: W3C · version: WCAG 2.2, W3C Recommendation 12 December 2024, https://www.w3.org/TR/2024/REC-WCAG22-20241212/, with its document Understanding SC 1.4.13 as updated 12 July 2026 · locator: Success Criterion 1.4.13 Content on Hover or Focus, and in Understanding SC 1.4.13 the sections Dismissible and Additional Notes
- **Media: the voice by part, the pause a button and the Space key, the tools of a selection** · in force as the decision of the repository's owner; its sources were found by a search of the literature, and none of them compares one player at the top of a page with a button at each part, counts the pauses a click on running text sets by mistake, or measures the cost of a comment window that opens on a selection unasked · the Space key, the pause when the cursor enters a field for the reader's words, the comment anchored to a piece of the text and the voice that goes on from the start of the interrupted sentence stand as they stood on the line `Media: the pause is where the reader looks; a piece of the text takes a comment` (§ Clauses that left), and the sentence on the clipboard stands on the line `Page: the clipboard is written only at a command of the reader` · Rey, Beege, Nebel, Wirzberger, Schmitt & Schneider *A Meta-analysis of the Segmenting Effect* doi:10.1007/s10648-018-9456-4, read in the copy at https://maria-wirzberger.de/wp-content/uploads/2019/01/Rey2019_Article_AMeta-analysisOfTheSegmentingE.pdf, Abstract, § Sample Characteristics, Table 3, § The Overall Segmentation Effect, Discussion — over 56 investigations and 88 comparisons of segmented against continuous multimedia instruction, "a learner-paced segmentation only fostered a significant increase in transfer performance": transfer d = 0.45 [0.24, 0.66] (k = 16), against retention d = 0.19 [−0.04, 0.45] (k = 21) and cognitive load d = 0.08, neither significant, while segments the system paced gave d = 0.42 on retention, 0.35 on transfer and 0.29 on cognitive load, segmenting lengthened learning time (d = −0.92 overall), and the homogeneity statistic was highly significant (retention Q = 2093.94, df = 66, p < 0.001); participants of a mean age of 19.71 years, multimedia instruction on natural science or mechanics in 39 of the comparisons, and segments an instructional designer cut, which the learner paces, rather than parts of a page the reader can also read in silence; Hasler, Kersten & Sweller *Learner control, cognitive load and instructional animation* doi:10.1002/acp.1345, read in the copy at https://www.runi.ac.il/media/j2jbwbjw/learnercontrolcognitiveloadandinstructional.pdf, Summary, Method p. 718, Results pp. 720–722 — 72 boys aged 9 to 11 in four groups of 18 learned about day and night from a continuous animation, a narration alone, 11 segments of about 19 s that stopped until Play was clicked, or an animation with free stop and play buttons; the two learner-paced groups scored higher than the two system-paced groups, on the questions of high element interactivity only, and rated their cognitive load lower, the ratings of the four groups differing by no significant amount (F(3, 68) = 0.91, p = 0.44), "In the stop-play group, 11 out of 18 students did not use the ‘stop’ and ‘play’ buttons at all", and in the segments group 10 children had to be told to press Play after the first segment; children and one short animation; Abrams & Christ *Motion onset captures attention* doi:10.1111/1467-9280.01458, read through the Europe PMC record at https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=TITLE:%22motion%20onset%20captures%20attention%22&resultType=core&format=json, abstract — in three visual-search experiments, the abstract giving no sample size, an object that had just started to move was identified with an advantage although its motion was uninformative, while letters that kept moving among static ones had none, and a replication with isoluminant stimuli by Guo, Abrams, Moscovitch & Pratt 2010, read by its abstract, found a very similar pattern; laboratory search for letters, not reading, so whether a control that jumps to each spoken sentence draws the eyes off the text is untested · Apple *Human Interface Guidelines*, Edit menus https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/edit-menus, read through https://developer.apple.com/tutorials/data/design/human-interface-guidelines/edit-menus.json, Platform considerations for iOS and iPadOS, Best practices — "Depending on available space, the default menu position is above or below the insertion point or selection.", the menu revealed by interactions people already know and its custom commands given short labels; native controls and not a web page, and on macOS the commands sit in a context menu and the menu bar; Google *Material Design 1*, Selection https://m1.material.io/patterns/selection.html § Text selection toolbar — "Actions related to the text appear in a dropdown menu positioned immediately above, but not overlapping, the selection."; an archived page on touch selection on Android; Hypothesis *Annotation Basics in the Hypothesis LMS App* https://web.hypothes.is/help/annotation-basics-in-the-hypothesis-lms-app/ § Creating notes — a selection shows a small menu of two buttons, Annotate and Highlight, and the card for a note opens only at Annotate; a description of a tool's behaviour, not evidence that it does better than a window that opens unasked; W3C *Understanding SC 3.2.2 On Input* (WCAG 2.2) https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/on-input.html Intent and the definition of change of context — opening a window and moving focus to a different component are changes of context, and "A change of content is not always a change of context."; selecting text is not changing the setting of a component, so the criterion bears on a window opened by a selection by analogy only; freedesktop.org *Clipboards* https://wiki.freedesktop.org/Specifications/ClipboardsWiki/ — "selecting but with no explicit copy should only set PRIMARY, never CLIPBOARD"; W3C *Clipboard API and events* https://www.w3.org/TR/clipboard-apis/ §9.2.1, §10 — a script's write is gated on transient activation or a permission, and an automated copy of something that was never selected is among the security concerns; MDN Web Docs *Clipboard API* https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Clipboard_API § Security considerations — Chromium accepts a write with the permission or transient activation, Firefox and Safari with transient activation alone; W3C *Understanding SC 2.5.2 Pointer Cancellation* (WCAG 2.2) https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/pointer-cancellation.html Intent — "to make it easier for users to prevent accidental or erroneous pointer input", while the criterion itself asks only that a function not complete on the down-event or that it can be aborted, undone or reversed, which a click on text that toggles the voice on the up-event can meet; W3C *ARIA Authoring Practices Guide*, Button Pattern https://www.w3.org/WAI/ARIA/apg/patterns/button/ Keyboard Interaction — "Space: Activates the button.", in a list for a button that has focus, a toggle button carrying aria-pressed; the guide has no pattern for a media player; YouTube Help *Keyboard shortcuts* https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/7631406?hl=en, rows Spacebar and k — "Play/Pause when the seek bar is selected. Activate a button if a button has focus.", the key k pausing and playing in the player; a video player, not a page of text whose Space scrolls; W3C *Understanding SC 2.1.4 Character Key Shortcuts* (WCAG 2.2) https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/character-key-shortcuts.html — the criterion covers shortcuts of printable characters, so a page-wide Space falls outside it, and a screen reader in browse mode passes Space and Enter to the page; that Space as the pause takes Space from scrolling is an inference no source measures, and the clause leaves Space to scroll until the reader starts the voice; Nielsen *10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design* https://www.nngroup.com/articles/ten-usability-heuristics/ #3 User Control and Freedom — "Users often perform actions by mistake.", a heuristic that gives no measured cost of such an action; W3C *Understanding SC 2.2.2 Pause, Stop, Hide* (WCAG 2.2) https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/pause-stop-hide.html — the criterion covers moving content that starts automatically, lasts more than five seconds and is presented in parallel with other content, its aim being "to avoid distracting users during their interaction with a web page."; movement that results from the reader's own activation falls outside its letter, so it bears on a control that travels with the spoken sentence by its aim only · kind: decision · owner: the repository owner · goal: the reader starts the voice where he reads and stops it without leaving the text, and selecting text does nothing he did not ask for · hypothesis: H-Media-the-voice-by-part-the-pause-a-button-and-the-Space-key-the-tools-of-a-selection
- **Page: the clipboard is written only at a command of the reader** · in force as the decision of the repository's owner, which takes the rule below as written; the sources were found by a search of the literature · freedesktop.org *Clipboards* specification https://wiki.freedesktop.org/Specifications/ClipboardsWiki/, the guidelines for application authors and the rationale that follows them — "selecting but with no explicit copy should only set PRIMARY, never CLIPBOARD", the older model of Qt 2 and Emacs 20 being rejected because a selection there overwrites the clipboard; a convention of the X11 desktop, and a web page cannot set PRIMARY, so the page keeps the half that concerns the clipboard; W3C *Clipboard API and events*, Working Draft, https://www.w3.org/TR/clipboard-apis/ §9.2.1 check clipboard write permission, §10 Security Considerations — a script's write is gated on a permission or on transient activation, "Let hasGesture be true if the relevant global object of this has transient activation, false otherwise.", and §10 names an automated copy of something that was never selected among its concerns; the specification governs what a browser permits, not what a design recommends, and whether a write at the end of a long drag still has activation in each browser is unverified; MDN Web Docs *Clipboard API* https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Clipboard_API § Security considerations — in Chromium "Writing requires either the clipboard-write permission or transient activation.", while Firefox and Safari require transient activation and do not support the permission, and a write works only in a secure context; the button that copies the piece is the command that gives the write its activation · kind: norm · issuer: freedesktop.org · version: the ClipboardsWiki page at https://wiki.freedesktop.org/Specifications/ClipboardsWiki/, last edited 2013-07-14 · locator: main body, the guidelines for application authors and the rationale for their behaviour
- **Media: a map of the page lists every part with its figures and stands open beside the text in a wide window** · in force as the decision of the repository's owner; its sources were found by a search of the literature, none of them tests a list of a page's figures as a panel to find one's way by, and none, standard or study, speaks of the page remembering whether the panel stands open · Pernice & Budiu (Nielsen Norman Group) *Hamburger Menus and Hidden Navigation Hurt UX Metrics* https://www.nngroup.com/articles/hamburger-menus/ the summary, § Why Study Hidden and Visible Navigation, § Measures, § Navigation Usage, § Hidden Navigation Leads to Worse User Experience, § Why Hidden Navigation Is Less Effective — 179 participants of a quantitative study run with a company of remote usability testing, on the desktop and phone versions of 6 live websites, the type of navigation going with the site, used the navigation on desktops in 27 % of cases when it was hidden against 48 % when visible and 50 % when partly visible, and on phones in 57 % against 86 % partly visible, the differences significant in a mixed-effects logistic regression with no effect size or interval given; on desktops they reached hidden navigation 5–7 s later (p < 0.1) and took at least 39 % longer over their tasks, and over both devices content discoverability was more than 20 % lower with hidden navigation and the rated difficulty 21 % higher than with visible navigation; the authors add that on desktops hidden navigation varies widely in place and label; the main navigation of live websites to find pages, not an outline of one page, the hiding mattering less for content an in-page link reaches; Dee-Lucas & Larkin *Learning From Electronic Texts: Effects of Interactive Overviews for Information Access* doi:10.1207/s1532690xci1303_4 abstract, read in its OpenAlex record https://api.openalex.org/works/doi:10.1207/s1532690xci1303_4 — a structured overview, a hierarchical map, and an unstructured content listing both gave better memory for the text's topics and broader recall than continuous text, the listing leaving a more fragmented knowledge structure without specific study goals and the structured overview being easier to recall and use, the abstract naming no sample; electronic text studied for learning and reached through an interactive overview, not a panel beside a continuous page; Cojean & Jamet *Does an interactive table of contents promote learning from videos? A study of consultation strategies and learning outcomes* doi:10.1111/bjet.13164 abstract, read in its OpenAlex record https://api.openalex.org/works/doi:10.1111/bjet.13164 — 57 higher-education students given a video with no table of contents, a static one or an interactive one with a cued timeline recalled more topics with a table, showed no significant effect on memorization or comprehension, and did not actively use the interactive table, reviewing by the timeline; a timed video, close to a list of parts inside a player; Amadieu, Tricot & Mariné *Prior knowledge in learning from a non-linear electronic document: Disorientation and coherence of the reading sequences* https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7126386/ Abstract, § 2.1, § 2.2.1, § 3.1, § 3.2.1 — future biology teachers (54 volunteers in § 2.1, 44 in the abstract) learning a virus multiplication cycle from 17 text sections of 681 words through a hierarchical or a network conceptual map showed an interaction of structure and prior knowledge on free recall (F(1, 50) = 5.11, p < .05, Cohen's f = 0.32): readers of low prior knowledge recalled more with the hierarchical map (F(1, 50) = 5.62, p < .05), readers of high prior knowledge showed no effect of structure on recall (F(1, 50) < 1), and the network map raised disorientation in both groups (F(1, 50) = 17.23, p < .001), the abstract adding that the hierarchical map reduced the disorientation of readers of low prior knowledge; two structures of a map rather than a map against none, in a short biology hypertext; Cuddihy & Spyridakis, Computers in Human Behavior 28(4):1399–1409, doi:10.1016/j.chb.2012.03.002, not opened and known through Benoliel & Becher *Messy Contracts* https://illinoislawreview.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/07/Benoliel.pdf p. 908 and notes 92–95, a relay — readers of a web article, of which the relay quotes the study at p. 1403, "The article spanned eight web pages and contained approximately 8,500 words", comprehended about 10 % more with a graphically distinct table of contents in the left-hand menu than with the original navigation of the whole site; an article spread over eight web pages rather than one page; Nielsen Norman Group *Table of Contents: The Ultimate Design Guide* https://www.nngroup.com/articles/table-of-contents/ § Placement Considerations, § Sticky vs. Nonsticky — in testing "many users failed to notice the sticky table of contents" where a mobile layout collapsed a left-rail table of contents into a sticky bar at the top of the page, with no count and the testing not described, so a relay, and the guide advises highlighting the current section of a sticky table of contents to show readers where they are, a practice report without measurement; Nielsen Norman Group *In-Page Links for Content Navigation* https://www.nngroup.com/articles/in-page-links-content-navigation/ § Research Findings: User Perception of In-Page Links — in a study of how 11 participants read long content pages, first exploring a page and then given directed tasks, users tended to skip in-page links while exploring and clicked them once they had a specific information need, and 2 of the 11 showed no engagement with them; consumer pages such as a smartphone review, without counts of the skipping; Hubbard & Dunbar *Perceptions of scientific research literature and strategies for reading papers depend on academic career stage* doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0189753 Results, Fig 2B, § Experienced researchers recommend a selective approach to scientific reading — in a survey of biological sciences at one UK university, undergraduates ranked the abstract and postgraduate researchers the figures and tables as the most important section of a paper; in the free-text advice of 88 researchers to someone reading a paper for the first time figures was the second most common word (n = 26), and 6 of them advised starting with the figures and 16 with the abstract; self-report on research papers; Shiely, Gallagher & Millar *How, and why, science and health researchers read scientific (IMRAD) papers* doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0297034 Materials and methods, Results — of 152 responses, 139 analysed on reading order, 98.6 % read the abstract first and only 15 read the results, as text or as figures and tables, second; self-report of science and health researchers and of MSc and PhD students; Kim, Hoque, Kim & Agrawala *Facilitating Document Reading by Linking Text and Tables* (UIST 2018) https://hci.stanford.edu/publications/2018/text-table-ref/paper.pdf Interactive Document Reader, User Study, Results — 14 adults from three academic institutions matched sentences to table cells with an accuracy of 73.1 % against 46.7 % (t(13) = 7.57, p < 0.001) and in 37.5 s against 48.6 s (t(13) = 4.12, p < 0.05) when the reader highlighted the cells a sentence refers to than in a baseline reader without the highlighting; each trial showed one sentence with its table, so the test measures the highlighted links and not the reader's side panel, which keeps the table most relevant to the text in view, and matching cells rather than finding one; Wang, Cachola, Bragg et al. *Improving the accessibility of scientific documents* https://arxiv.org/pdf/2105.00076v1 § 4, the prototype versions v0.1–v0.3, and § 6, Table 6 — a pilot participant who reads with the NVDA screen reader lost his reading location when he navigated away to the figures, which v0.1 placed in a section after the body, and the designers moved figures and tables next to their first mention and added a table of contents; one participant of a study of blind and low-vision researchers, no numbers; Head, Lo, Kang et al. *Augmenting Scientific Papers with Just-in-Time, Position-Sensitive Definitions of Terms and Symbols* (CHI 2021) https://andrewhead.info/assets/pdf/augmenting-scientific-papers.pdf § 3.3 design motive M5, § 4.1, § 6 Usability Study, § 7 Discussion — in a usability study of 27 researchers 10 opened the list of a term's usages, which opens in a sidebar rather than in a tooltip so as not to cover the text, readers of earlier prototypes having reported tooltips covering it, and one of them described the list as a guide to non-linear reading; the discussion adds readers skimming a section by jumping from one equation diagram to the next; observations, not measurements, of a list of usages rather than of figures; Katz & Byrne *Effects of Scent and Breadth on Use of Site-Specific Search on E-Commerce Web Sites* (ACM TOCHI 10(3)) doi:10.1145/937549.937551 abstract (OpenAlex record) — in an exploratory study the use of site search did not yield faster or more accurate performance in locating products, and in a second experiment with novel e-commerce sites browsing was influenced by both the breadth and the information scent of the menus, with no figures in the abstract; site search against menus, not a filter over a visible list; Bailly, Oulasvirta, Brumby & Howes *Model of Visual Search and Selection Time in Linear Menus* (CHI 2014) https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/1431982/1/Brumby_model_of_visual_search_and_selection.pdf Menu Performance Phenomena, item 1, and the results, Menu length effect — the paper lists from earlier studies that the time to find the same target position grows with the number of items in a menu, and its experiment with 21 participants and menus of 8, 12 and 16 items replicates it (F(2, 40) = 70.44, p < .001), the effect shrinking with practice; menus, not a map of a page; Salmerón & García *Children's Reading of Printed Text and Hypertext with Navigation Overviews: The Role of Comprehension, Sustained Attention, and Visuo-spatial Abilities* https://www.uv.es/lasalgon/papers/2012_JECR.pdf Abstract, Method, Discussion — of 66 sixth-grade students, those of low sustained attention scored higher on questions that integrate information in a hypertext with a navigable graphical overview than in print, the hypothesis being only partly supported; children, the author's manuscript · Apple *Human Interface Guidelines*, Sidebars https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/sidebars, read through https://developer.apple.com/tutorials/data/design/human-interface-guidelines/sidebars.json, Best practices — "Avoid hiding the sidebar by default to ensure that it remains discoverable.", a show/hide button or commands of the View menu on macOS, a sidebar that may hide and return as its window resizes, succinct descriptive labels for two levels and in general no more than two levels of hierarchy; native app navigation, not an outline of a web page; Material Components for Android *Navigation drawers* https://raw.githubusercontent.com/material-components/material-components-android/master/docs/components/NavigationDrawer.md — a standard drawer lets the reader use the content and the drawer at once and does not suit phones, a modal drawer for phones blocks the content with a scrim and is elevated above most of the app's interface without changing the layout grid, and the drawer is being deprecated for an expanded navigation rail in the Material 3 expressive update; *Top app bar* https://raw.githubusercontent.com/material-components/material-components-android/master/docs/components/TopAppBar.md Anatomy — the navigation button is the "Leading button" at the start of the bar, the left edge in a left-to-right script; app navigation, not an outline of a page; Android Developers *Use window size classes* https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/layouts/adaptive/use-window-size-classes — width classes compact below 600dp, medium from 600dp to 840dp, expanded from 840dp to 1200dp, and large and extra large above it, width mattering more than height; a dp is not a CSS pixel, and the source names no drawer for each class; CSS Scroll Anchoring Module Level 1, Editor's Draft of 30 September 2026, https://drafts.csswg.org/css-scroll-anchoring/ §2, §2.2.2 — the browser "attempts to keep the user’s view of the document stable across layout changes" by an anchor node, but a change of width, margin, padding or position on the path from the anchor to the scroller suppresses the adjustment, which is the usual way a panel that pushes the text narrows it, so the page keeps the reader's place itself; MDN browser-compat-data https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mdn/browser-compat-data/main/css/properties/overflow-anchor.json — overflow-anchor from Chrome 56, Firefox 66 and Safari 27; web.dev *Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS)* https://web.dev/articles/cls — shifts within 500 ms of a user's input are left out of the metric, the page naming readers who lose their place when text moves suddenly, a performance metric and not evidence that a reflow the reader starts keeps him oriented; W3C *Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG) 2.2* https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/ SC 1.4.10 Reflow — content is presented without loss and without scrolling in two dimensions at a width equivalent to 320 CSS pixels, which leaves a narrow window to lay the panel over the text or stack it; SC 2.4.6 Headings and Labels — "Headings and labels describe topic or purpose.", with no length set; SC 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured (Minimum) — a component with keyboard focus is not entirely hidden by author-created content, which a panel left open over the text has to respect; SC 2.4.5 Multiple Ways concerns locating a page within a set of pages and asks for no panel inside one page; W3C *WAI-ARIA 1.2*, Recommendation of 06 June 2023, https://www.w3.org/TR/wai-aria-1.2/ aria-current — "Authors SHOULD only mark one element in a set of elements as current with aria-current.", the attribute exposing the current item of a set and saying nothing of whether marking it helps readers; W3C WAI *ARIA Authoring Practices Guide*, Landmark Regions https://www.w3.org/WAI/ARIA/apg/practices/landmark-regions/ § Navigation — a page with more than one navigation landmark gives each a unique label; W3C *HTML Accessibility API Mappings 1.0*, Working Draft of 26 October 2026, https://www.w3.org/TR/html-aam-1.0/ §4.1.9 — a figcaption takes no part in a figure's accessible name unless the author references it, the name coming from aria-label, aria-labelledby or title; Nielsen Norman Group *Anchors OK? Re-Assessing In-Page Links* https://www.nngroup.com/articles/in-page-links/ § Sticky Links: Show the User's Location, § Confirm the Jump — a list of in-page links that stays in place as the reader scrolls should show his current location, and sticky elements must not cover the heading a jump lands on, guidance without measurement · kind: decision · owner: the repository owner · goal: a reader who returns to the page reaches a figure, a table or a question without scrolling through the parts, and sees where he is · hypothesis: H-Media-a-map-of-the-page-lists-every-part-with-its-figures-and-stands-open-beside-the-text-in-a-wide-window
